Regular polytopes of rank n / 2 n/2 for transitive groups of degree n n
Maria Elisa Fernandes, Claudio Alexandre Piedade

Abstract
. Previous research established that the maximal rank of the abstract regular polytopes whose automorphism group is a transitive proper subgroup of S n \mathrm{S}_{n} is n / 2 + 1 n/2+1 . Up to isomorphism and duality, when n ≥ 12 n\geq 12 , there are only two polytopes attaining this rank and they occur when n / 2 n/2 is odd, and hence have even rank. In this paper, we investigate the case where the rank is equal to n / 2 n/2 ( n ≥ 14 n\geq 14 ). Our analysis suggests that reducing the rank by one results in a substantial increase in the number of regular polytopes.



Keywords:
Abstract Regular Polytopes; String C-Groups; Symmetric Groups; Alternating Groups; Permutation Groups.

2000 Math Subj. Class:
52B11, 20B35, 20B30, 05C25.

1.
Introduction

Abstract polytopes are combinatorial objects that describe standard regular polytopes using their a face-lattice
[
14
]
. The rank of an abstract polytope is the length of a maximal chain minus two (there exists a maximal face and a minimal face which are not counted for the rank).
An abstract polytope is regular when its group of automorphisms acts regularly on the maximal chains (usually called flags).
A notable feature of these structures lies in their one to one correspondence with their automorphism groups, which are string C-groups.
These algebraic structures are defined not only by the group itself but also by a specified set of involutory generators, the size of which determines the rank.

The maximal rank of an abstract regular polytope whose automorphism group has degree
n
is
n-1
.
For
n\geq 5
, the simplex is the only polytope achieving the maximal rank
[
8
,
9
]
. One permutation representation of the group of automorphism of the simplex on
n
points is

\langle(1,2),\,(2,3),\,\ldots,(n-1,n)\rangle
, the polytope with Schläfli symbol
\{3,\ldots,3\}
corresponding to the standard Coxeter group of type
\mathrm{A}_{n-1}
.
Indeed
n-1
is the maximal size of an independent set in
\mathrm{S}_{n}
, and
\mathrm{S}_{n}
is the unique group of degree
n
that admits an independent set of generators of size
n-1
[
15
]
. The classification of abstract regular polytopes of ranks
n-k
for groups of degree
n\geq 2k+3
, was also accomplished for
k\in\{1,2,3,4\}
in
[
10
]
.
The automorphism group of all these high-rank abstract polytopes is
\mathrm{S}_{n}
. The rank of a regular polytope with automorphism group being an alternating group is considerably lower than
n-1
. In fact, the highest rank for the alternating group
\mathrm{A}_{n}
is
\lfloor(n-1)/2\rfloor
for
n\geq 12

[
3
]
.

The analyses of the
C-rank
(maximal size of a set of generators of a string C-group) of other transitive permutation groups of degree
n
, different from
\mathrm{A}_{n}
and
\mathrm{S}_{n}
, started in
[
4
]
. The C-rank of a primitive proper subgroup of
\mathrm{S}_{n}
is at most
n/2+1
. Indeed, only few of these groups have C-rank greater than or equal
n/2
(see Table 2 of
[
4
]
).

In this paper we give the classification of the transitive proper subgroups of
\mathrm{S}_{n}
with C-rank greater than or equal to
n/2
(
n\geq 14
), refining the classification given in
[
4
]
.
The group generated by the set
S=\{\rho_{0},\ldots,\rho_{r-1}\}
of permutations of degree
n
will be represented by a graph, which is a slight modification of the Schreier coset digraph,
where each pair of opposite arcs with label
\rho_{i}
are replaced by a single edge with label
i
. For the maximal possible rank
r=n/2+1
, we noticed that in Theorem 1.2 (b) of
[
4
]
there is a possibility for the set of generators that is missing, namely the one having the permutation representation graph (2) of Table
5
.

The main result of the paper is the following.

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
n/2\geq 7
and
G
be a transitive proper subgroup of
\mathrm{S}_{n}
. If
G
is the automorphism group of an abstract regular polytope of rank
r\geq n/2
, then
G
is one of the following groups.

\textrm{C}_{2}\times\mathrm{S}_{n/2},\,(\textrm{C}_{2})^{n/2-1}:\mathrm{S}_{n/2},\,(\textrm{C}_{2})^{n/2}:\mathrm{S}_{n/2}\mbox{ or }(\mathrm{S}_{n/2})^{2}:\textrm{C}_{2}.

More precisely, all the possibilities for the set generators
S=\{\rho_{0},\ldots,\rho_{r-1}\}
of
G
are, up to duality, included among the graphs of the tables displayed in Section
6
.

Conjecture 1.2
.

The graphs given in Tables (
5
)–(
9
) are permutation representation graphs of string C-groups.

The paper is organized as follows.
The results in Section
2
allow us to reduce our study to the case where
G
is a transitive imprimitive group.
In Section
3
, we further reduce our analysis to two cases: either
G
is a transitive imprimitive group with a block system consisting of
n/2
blocks of size two, or with two blocks of size
n/2
.
These two cases are treated separately in Sections
4
and
5
, respectively. Finally, in Section
6
, we present a proof of Theorem
1.1
.

2.
Preliminaries

The automorphism group of an abstract regular polytope is a string C-group. One important fact about string C-groups is the one-to-one correspondence between abstract regular polytopes and string C-groups
[
14
]
. Hence, a classification of string C-groups results in a classification of abstract regular polytopes. Before introducing the formal definition of a string C-group, we first present some preliminary results on independent sets and string groups, which are closely related concepts.

2.1.
Independent generating sets

Definition 2.1
.

Let
G
be a group. A set
S=\{\rho_{0},\ldots,\rho_{r-1}\}
is an
independent generating set
of
G
if
\rho_{i}\notin\langle\rho_{j}\,|\,j\neq i\rangle
and
G=\langle S\rangle
.

One important result of Whiston
[
15
]
regards the maximal size of an independent generating set of the symmetric group of degree
n
.

Theorem 2.2
.

[
15
, Theorem 1]

The maximal size of an independent generating set for a group of degree
n
is
n-1
. Moreover
\mathrm{S}_{n}
is the only group having an independent generating set of size
n-1
.

In what follows let
T
be a tree with
n
vertices and let
S(T)
be the set of transpositions corresponding to the edges of
T
.

Theorem 2.3
.

[
1
, Theorem 2.1]
Let
S
be an independent generating set for
\mathrm{S}_{n}
of size
n-1
, where

n\geq 7
. Then there is a tree
T
on
\{1,\ldots,n\}
such that one of the following holds:

(a)

S=S(T)
;

(b)

for some element
s\in S(T)
, we have

S=\{s\}\cup\{(st)^{\epsilon(t)}\,:\,t\in S(T)\setminus\{s\}\}\mbox{ where }\epsilon(t)=\pm 1.

Conversely, each of these sets is an independent generating set for
\mathrm{S}_{n}
.

2.2.
Sggi’s and permutation representation graphs

Definition 2.4
.

A
string group generated by involutions
or, for short, a
sggi
is a pair
\Gamma=(G,S)
where
G=\langle S\rangle
with
S=\{\rho_{0},\ldots,\rho_{r-1}\}
being an ordered set of involutions
that satisfy the following property, called the commuting property.

\forall i,j\in\{0,\ldots,r-1\},\;|i-j|>1\Rightarrow(\rho_{i}\rho_{j})^{2}=1.

The
dual of a sggi
is obtained by reversing the sequence of generators (take the ordering induced by the indices of the elements in the generating set).

When
G
is a group of degree
n
, a sggi
(G,S)
can be represented by its permutation representation graph, defined as follows.

Definition 2.5
.

Suppose that
G
is a permutation group of degree
n
and let
\Gamma=(G,\{\rho_{0},\ldots,\rho_{r-1}\})
be a sggi. The
permutation representation graph

\mathcal{G}
of
\Gamma
is an
r
-edge-labelled multigraph with
n
vertices and with an
i
-edge

\{a,\,b\}
whenever
a\rho_{i}=b
with
a\neq b
and
i\in\{0,\ldots,r-1\}
.

To avoid some cumbersome notation, we introduce the following notation that will be heavely used in the following sections.

Notation 2.6
.

Let us consider the following notation.

\begin{array}[]{ll}I_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}:=\{0,\ldots,r-1\}\setminus\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}\}&I^{\leq i}:=\{0,\ldots,i\}\qquad I^{\geq i}:=\{i,\ldots,r-1\}\\[5.0pt]
I^{<i}:=\{0,\ldots,i-1\}&I^{>i}:=\{i+1,\ldots,r-1\}\\[5.0pt]
I^{\leq i}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}:=\{0,\ldots,i\}\setminus\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}\}&I^{\geq i}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}:=\{i,\ldots,r-1\}\setminus\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}\}\\[5.0pt]
I^{<i}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}:=\{0,\ldots,i-1\}\setminus\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}\}&I^{>i}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}:=\{i+1,\ldots,r-1\}\setminus\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}\}\\[5.0pt]
G_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}:=\langle\rho_{i}\,|\,i\in I_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}\rangle&G_{\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}\}}:=\langle\rho_{i}\,|\,i\in\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}\}\rangle\\[5.0pt]
G_{<i}:=\langle\rho_{j}\,|\,j\in I^{<i}\rangle&G_{>i}:=\langle\rho_{j}\,|\,j\in I^{>i}\rangle\\[5.0pt]
\Gamma_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}:=(G_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}},\{\rho_{j}\,|\,j\in I_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}\})&\Gamma_{\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}\}}:=(G_{\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}\}},\{\rho_{j}\,|\,j\in\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}\}\});\\[5.0pt]
\Gamma_{<i}:=(G_{<i},\{\rho_{0},\ldots,\rho_{i-1}\})\qquad(i\neq 0)&\Gamma_{>i}:=(G_{>i},\{\rho_{i+1},\ldots,\rho_{r-1}\})\qquad(i\neq r-1);\\[5.0pt]
\end{array}

Let
\mathcal{G}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}
(resp.
\mathcal{G}_{\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}\}}
) denote the permutation representation graph of
\Gamma_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}
(resp.
\Gamma_{\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}\}}
).

Notice that when
\rho_{i}
is a
k
-transposition (a product of
k
disjoint transpositions),
\mathcal{G}_{\{i\}}
is a matching with
k
edges.
A consequence of the commuting property (see Definition
2.4
) is that, if
i
and
j
are nonconsecutive the connected components of
\mathcal{G}_{\{i,j\}}
with more than two vertices are
\{i,j\}
-squares
(squares with alternating labels
i,\,j,\,i,\,j
). A
J
-edge
is a set of
|J|
parallel edges with label-set
J
. Sometimes we represent these set of edges by a single edge with the label
J
.

The following lemma lists all sggi’s of rank at least
n-3
associated with primitive groups of degree
n
, other than
\mathrm{S}_{n}
and
\mathrm{A}_{n}
.

.

Let
G
be a primitive subgroup of
\mathrm{S}_{n}
not equal to
\mathrm{A}_{n}
or
\mathrm{S}_{n}
. Let
S
be an independent generating set of size
r
for
G
such that
(G,S)
is an sggi.
If
r\geq n-3
then
G
is one of the groups in the following table.

, for

,

.

.

\mathrm{lcs}(G)
denotes the size of a longest chain of subgroups of
G

We now provide a rank bound for some particular sggi’s related to imprimitive groups.

Lemma 2.8
.

Let
\Gamma=(G,S)
is a sggi where
G
is a transitive subgroup of
\mathrm{S}_{n}
.
Suppose
G
satisfies the following conditions:

(a)

S=\{\rho_{0},\ldots,\rho_{r-1}\}
is independent;

(b)

G_{r-1}
is intransitive; and

(c)

G_{j}
is transitive for some
j\notin\{0,r-1\}
.

If
j
is the maximal label satisfying (c),
k
is the size of each
G_{<j}
-orbit and
m=n/k
, then
G_{j}\leq\mathrm{S}_{k}\wr\mathrm{S}_{m}
and

r-1\leq(k-1)+(m-1).

Proof.

As
G_{r-1}
is intransitive,
G_{<j}
is intransitive.
Hence, as
G_{j}=G_{<j}\times G_{>j}
, there is a block system for
G_{j}
where the blocks are the
G_{<j}
-orbits. Let
k
be the size of a
G_{<j}
-orbit and
m
be the number of
G_{<j}
-orbits. It follows that
G_{j}\leq\mathrm{S}_{k}\wr\mathrm{S}_{m}
.
Since
G_{<j}\leq\mathrm{S}_{k}
is generated by
j
elements, it follows that
j\leq k-1
.
As
j
is the maximal label satisfying (c),
G_{i}
is intransitive for
i>j
.
Thus, for each
i>j
there exists a pair of
G_{<j}
-orbits that belong to different
G_{i}
-orbits.
Consider a graph whose vertices are the
G_{<j}
-orbits having exactly one edge
i
between
G_{<j}
-orbits that belong to different
G_{i}
-orbits.
The graph is a forest with
m
vertices and
(r-1)-j
edges. Hence
(r-1)-j\leq m-1
.
Consequently
r-1\leq(k-1)+(m-1)
, as wanted.
∎

2.3.
String C-groups

Definition 2.9
.

A
string C-group of rank
r
, denoted as
\Gamma=(G,S)
, is a sggi which satisfies the following property called the
intersection property
:

\forall J,K\subseteq\{0,\ldots,r-1\},\langle\rho_{j}\mid j\in J\rangle\cap\langle\rho_{k}\mid k\in K\rangle=\langle\rho_{j}\mid j\in J\cap K\rangle.

If
p_{i}
is the order of
\rho_{i-1}\rho_{i}
,
i=1,\ldots,r-1
, the string C-group
G
is the group of automorphisms of an abstract regular polytope with
Schläfli symbol

\{p_{1},\ldots,p_{r-1}\}
.

The set
S
in the previous definition is an independent generating set for
G
. An immediate consequence of Theorem
2.3
is the following:

Corollary 2.10
.

Let
\Gamma=(G,\{\rho_{0},\ldots,\rho_{r-1}\})
be a sggi of degree
n
generated by independent involutions.
If
r=n-1
and
n\geq 7
then
\Gamma
is a string C-group, namely it is the group of automorphisms of the
(n-1)
-simplex.

The following two theorems of
[
8
]
give the classification of the abstract regular polytopes, whose the automorphism group is a subgroup of
\mathrm{S}_{n}
, having rank
r\in\{n-1,n-2\}
.

Theorem 2.11
.

[
8
, Theorem 1]

For
n\geq 5
, the
(n-1)
-simplex is, up to isomorphism, the unique polytope of rank

n-1
having a group
\mathrm{S}_{n}
as automorphism group. For
n=4
, there are, up to isomorphism and
duality, two abstract regular polyhedra whose automorphism group is
\mathrm{S}_{4}
, namely the hemicube
and the tetrahedron. Finally, for
n=3
, there is, up to isomorphism, a unique abstract regular
polygon whose automorphism group is
\mathrm{S}_{3}
, namely the triangle.

Theorem 2.12
.

[
8
, Theorem 2]

For
n\geq 7
, there exists, up to isomorphism and duality, a unique
(n-2)
-polytope
having a group
\mathrm{S}_{n}
as automorphism group and Schläfli symbol
\{4,6,3,3,\ldots,3\}
.

In
[
8
]
and
[
9
]
the authors give the possible permutation representation graphs of the string C-groups of Theorems 1 and 2. We list them in the following proposition.

Proposition 2.13
.

The permutation representation graph of degree
n
of the group of automorphisms of the abstract regular polytopes of rank
r=n-1
(
n\geq 5
) is as follows:

\scriptstyle{0}
\scriptstyle{1}
\scriptstyle{r-1}

The permutation representation graph of degree
n
of the group of automorphisms of the abstract regular polytope of rank
r=n-2
(
n\geq 7
) is, up to duality, as follows:

\scriptstyle{1}
\scriptstyle{0}
\scriptstyle{1}
\scriptstyle{2}
\scriptstyle{r-1}

In
[
10
]
, the authors also provide a classification of abstract regular polytopes whose automorphism group is a subgroup of
\mathrm{S}_{n}
, with rank
r\in{n-3,n-4}
for sufficiently large
n
. One of the key consequences of their results is the following.

Proposition 2.14
.

Let
\Gamma=(G,S)
be a sggi and
x\in\{1,2,3,4\}
. If
G_{j}
is intransitive for all
j\in\{0,\ldots,r-1\}
,
r=n-x
and
n\geq 3+2x
, then
G\cong\mathrm{S}_{n}
.
Moreover if
x\in\{1,2\}
then
\Gamma
has, up to duality, one of the permutation representation graph given in Proposition
2.13
.

Proof.

We just need to observe that each sggi
G
having one of the permutation representation graphs of Table 2 of
[
10
]
is isomorphic to
\mathrm{S}_{n}
.
Indeed in each case we find a transposition
(a,b)\in G
such that the stabilizer of
a
is transitive of
\{1,\ldots,n\}\setminus\{a\}
. Hence
G\cong\mathrm{S}_{n}
.

In addition, when
x\in\{1,2\}
Table 2 of
[
10
]
gives only two possibilities for the permutation representation graph, precisely the ones of Proposition
2.13
.
∎

The goal of this paper is to extend this result to the case where the rank is exactly
r=n/2
.

2.4.
Permutation representation of string C-groups for
\mathrm{S}_{n}
of rank
r\in\{n-2,n-1\}
on
2n
points

There is a one-to-one correspondence between faithful transitive permutation representations of a group

G
and its core-free subgroup
1
1

1

H
is a core-free subgroup of
G
when
\cap_{g\in G}g^{-1}Hg
is the trivial.

[
7
, Section 1.3]
.
With this in mind, we show below that the automorphism group of the
(n-1)
-simplex, as well as that of the polytope of rank
n-2
having Schläfli type
\{4,6,3,\ldots,3\}
, admits a unique transitive permutation representation of degree

2n
when
n\geq 7
.

Proposition 2.18
.

Let
n\geq 7
. There exists exactly one faithful transitive permutation representation of
\mathrm{S}_{n}
on
2n
points.

Proof.

Let us prove that, up to conjugacy, there exists only one core-free subgroup of
\mathrm{S}_{n}
of index
2n
when
n\geq 7
.
Suppose that
H
is a subgroup of
\mathrm{S}_{n}
of index at most
2n
.
By the O’Nan-Scott Theorem
[
17
]
, we have one of the following possibilities for
H
:
(a)
H\leq\mathrm{S}_{a}\times\mathrm{S}_{n-a}
, for
1\leq a\leq n/2
; (b)
H\leq\mathrm{S}_{n/a}^{a}\rtimes\mathrm{S}_{a}
, for
a|n
and
1<a<n
;
or (c)
H
is a primitive subgroup of
\mathrm{S}_{n}
(different from
\mathrm{A}_{n}
and
\mathrm{S}_{n}
).

In case (a)
|H|\leq a!(n-a)!
, hence
\frac{n!}{a!(n-a)!}\leq 2n
, which is only possible if
a=1
.
Then
H\leq\mathrm{S}_{n-1}
and
|\mathrm{S}_{n-1}:H|=2
, which gives
H\cong\mathrm{A}_{n-1}
. Case (b) is never possible as
\frac{n!}{a!(n/a)!^{a}}>2n
, for
n\geq 7
.
In case (c) using the bound for a primitive group given in

[
18
]
,
|H|\leq 4^{n}
, meaning that
\frac{n!}{4^{n}}\leq 2n
,
which is only possible for
n\leq 11
. But then, using
[
19
]
we find no possibility for small degrees either.
∎

Notice that, when
n=6
, we find two faithful transitive permutation representation of
\mathrm{S}_{6}
on
12
points.

Proposition 2.19
.

If
\Gamma=(G,\{\rho_{0},\ldots,\rho_{r-1}\})
is a string C-group of rank
r\in\{n-1,n-2\}
and
G
is a transitive group of degree
2n
isomorphic to
\mathrm{S}_{n}
, then

\Gamma
has, up to duality, one the following permutation representations:

r=n-1:

\scriptstyle{I_{0}}
\scriptstyle{0}
\scriptstyle{I_{0,1}}
\scriptstyle{1}
\scriptstyle{I_{1,2}}
\scriptstyle{I_{n-3,n-2}}
\scriptstyle{n-2}
\scriptstyle{I_{n-2,n-1}}
\scriptstyle{n-1}
\scriptstyle{I_{n-1}}
\scriptstyle{0}
\scriptstyle{1}
\scriptstyle{n-2}
\scriptstyle{n-1}

r=n-2:

\scriptstyle{I_{1}}
\scriptstyle{1}
\scriptstyle{I_{0,1}}
\scriptstyle{0}
\scriptstyle{I_{0,1}}
\scriptstyle{1}
\scriptstyle{I_{1,2}}
\scriptstyle{2}
\scriptstyle{I_{1,2,3}}
\scriptstyle{I_{1,n-3,n-2}}
\scriptstyle{n-2}
\scriptstyle{I_{1,n-2,n-1}}
\scriptstyle{n-1}
\scriptstyle{I_{1,n-1}}
\scriptstyle{1}
\scriptstyle{0}
\scriptstyle{1}
\scriptstyle{2}
\scriptstyle{n-2}
\scriptstyle{n-1}

Proof.

Consider first that
\Gamma
is the string C-group of rank
n-1
, which is known as the
(n-1)
-simplex.
Consider the subgroup
H=\langle\rho_{1}\rho_{2},\rho_{2}\rho_{3},\ldots,\rho_{r-3}\rho_{r-2},\rho_{r-2}\rho_{r-1}\rangle
of
G_{0}
. This group is an index
2
subgroup of
G_{0}
, known as the rotational group of
\Gamma_{0}
.
As
G_{0}\cong\mathrm{S}_{n-1}
,
H\cong\mathrm{A}_{n-1}
.
By Proposition
2.18
we only need to compute the Schreier coset graph with respect to
H
. Using the Todd–Coxeter algorithm
2
2

2

An algorithm that enumerates the cosets of a subgroup in a finitely presented group and, in doing so, produces a permutation representation of the group’s generators acting on those cosets
[
11
]
.
we get the graph given in the statement of this proposition.

Now consider the case
r=n-2
. In this case
\Gamma
is the abstract regular polytope of Theorem
2.12
.
Up to duality
\Gamma
is the abstract regular polytope of with Schläfli symbol
\{4,6,3,\ldots,3\}
. Then
G_{r-1}\cong\mathrm{S}_{n-1}
and the rotational subgroup
H
of
G_{r-1}
is isomorphic to
\mathrm{A}_{n-1}
. Applying the Todd–Coxeter algorithm to
H
we obtain the second permutation representation given in the statement of this proposition.
∎

2.5.
Imprimitive groups with blocks of size
2
with block action isomorphic to
\mathrm{S}_{n/2}
or
\mathrm{A}_{n/2}

Mortimer, in
[
13
, Lemma 2]
, provides sufficient conditions under which all

G
-submodules over a field of characteristic 2 are among the following: the trivial submodule consisting of the all-zeros vector, the constant submodule generated by the all-ones vector, the even-weight submodule, and the full module. In particular, when

G
contains the alternating group of degree equal to the dimension of the module, the required conditions are satisfied. As a consequence, we obtain the following result.

Theorem 2.20
.

Let
n/2\geq 3
, Suppose that
G\leq\mathrm{C}_{2}\wr\mathrm{S}_{n/2}
and let
f:G\to\mathrm{S}_{n/2}
be the homomorphism induced by the action of

G
on the
n/2
blocks.
If
{\rm Im}(f)
is either
\mathrm{S}_{n/2}
or
\mathrm{A}_{n/2}
then
{\rm Ker}(f)
is either trivial or isomorphic to one of the groups:

\mathrm{C}_{2},\,(\mathrm{C}_{2})^{n/2-1}\mbox{ or }(\mathrm{C}_{2})^{n/2}.

Proof.

Let
n/2\geq 3
and
G
be either
\mathrm{S}_{n/2}
or
\mathrm{A}_{n/2}
.
By Lemma 2 of
[
13
]
there are only four
G
-modules over a field of characteristic
2
.
Each submodule is in one-to-one correspondence with the possible kernels of
f
, as follows:

Submodulo

\textrm{Ker}(f)

trivial submodule

trivial group

constant submodule

\mathrm{C}_{2}

even-weight submodule

(\mathrm{C}_{2})^{n/2-1}

full module

(\mathrm{C}_{2})^{n/2}

∎

The following lemma addresses the case in which the block action is the alternating group.

Lemma 2.21
.

Let
n\geq 6
and
2<l<n/2
. Suppose that
G
is a transitive subgroup of
\mathrm{C}_{2}\wr\mathrm{A}_{n/2}
and let
f:G\to\mathrm{A}_{n/2}
be the homomorphism induced by the action of
G
on the
n/2
blocks.
If
{\rm Im}(f)\cong\mathrm{A}_{n/2}
then the following holds:

(a)

If
G
contains a transposition fixing the blocks, then
{\rm Ker}(f)\cong(\mathrm{C}_{2})^{n}
;

(b)

If
G
contains a
2
-transposition fixing the blocks, then
(\mathrm{C}_{2})^{n-1}\leq{\rm Ker}(f)
; and

(c)

If
G
contains a
l
-transposition fixing the blocks, then
(\mathrm{C}_{2})^{n-1}\leq{\rm Ker}(f)
.

Proof.

Let
\tau\in G
be a permutation fixing the blocks. We will consider separately the cases: (a)
\tau
is a transposition, (b)
\tau
is a
2
-transposition and (c)
\tau
is a
(n/2-1)
-transposition.

(a) Let
B_{1}
be the block where
\tau
acts nontrivially. Let
B_{i}
be other block. There exist a permutation
g\in G
such that
gB_{1}=B_{i}
.
Moreover
\tau^{g}
is the transposition swapping the pairs of points of
B_{i}
. Hence
{\rm Ker}(f)\cong(\mathrm{C}_{2})^{n}
.

(b) As
\mathrm{A}_{n/2}
is
2
-transitive, any
2
-transposition fixing the blocks can be obtained by a conjugation of
\tau
.
Let
B_{1}
and
B_{2}
be the blocks where
\tau
acts nontrivially.
For any pair of
\{B_{i},B_{j}\}
there exist
g\in G
such that
\{B_{1},B_{2}\}g=\{B_{i},B_{j}\}
.
Hence
(\mathrm{C}_{2})^{n-1}\leq{\rm Ker}(f)
.

(c) Suppose first that
n/2
is odd. Let
\alpha\in G
be a product of two cycles of size
n/2
(permuting all blocks in a single cycle).
Then
\tau\tau^{\alpha}
is a
2
-transposition fixing the blocks.
Now suppose that
n/2
is even. Let
\mathrm{Fix}(\tau)=\{a,b\}
.
Let
\beta\in G
be a permutation that acts on the blocks as a cycle of size
n/2-1
that does not fix the block
\{a,b\}
, that is
\{a,b\}\beta\neq\{a,b\}
.
Then, as before,
\tau\tau^{\beta}
is a
2
-transposition fixing the blocks.
In both cases, by (b), we get
(\mathrm{C}_{2})^{n-1}\leq{\rm Ker}(f)
.
∎

Combining the results of Theorem
2.20
and Lemma
2.21
, we get the following theorem.

Theorem 2.22
.

Let
H
be either
\mathrm{A}_{n/2}
or
\mathrm{S}_{n/2}
and
n\geq 6
. If
G
is a transitive subgroup of degree
n
embedded into
\mathrm{C}_{2}\wr H
, then the following statements hold:

(a)

The index of
G
in
\mathrm{C}_{2}\wr H
is either
1,\,2,\,2^{n/2-1}\mbox{ or }2^{n/2}
;

(b)

If the index of
G
in
\mathrm{C}_{2}\wr H
is equal to
2^{n/2-1}
then
G
contains the permutation that swaps the two points in every block;

(c)

If the index of
G
in
\mathrm{C}_{2}\wr H
is equal to
2
then
G
contains all even permutations fixing the blocks; and

(d)

Let
n/2
be even. If
|G|=2|H|
then
G
is even.

Proof.

(a), (b) and (c) are immediate consequences of Theorem
2.20
, Lemma
2.21
and their proofs.

(d) In this case
G
contains the permutation
\alpha
swapping all pairs of points within the blocks.

Suppose first that
H=\mathrm{A}_{n/2}
.
As
n/2
is even
G
contains a permutation
\delta
that fixes exactly one block
B
and that permutes all the other blocks cyclically.
This permutation is odd if it acts nontrivially in
B
. In any case
\delta^{2}
is even, indeed it is written as a product of two
(n/2-1)
-cycles.
Now consider any block
X
and the set of three blocks
\{X,X\delta^{2},B\}
.
There exists a permutation
\beta
that permutes these blocks cyclically, fixes another block
Y
and swaps the remaining blocks pair wisely.
By construction
\beta^{4}
is a product of two
3
-cycles permuting the blocks
\{X,X\delta^{2},B\}
.
Hence
\langle\beta^{4},\delta^{2}\rangle
acts has
\mathrm{A}_{n/2}
on the blocks.
As
|G|=2|\mathrm{A}_{n/2}|
, then
G=\langle\beta^{4},\delta^{2},\alpha\rangle
, hence
G
is even, as wanted.

Now consider the case
H=\mathrm{S}_{n/2}
.
In this case
G
contains the permutation
\delta
that permutes all the other blocks cyclically and
\delta^{2}
is written as a product of two
n/2
-cycles.
As in the previous case given a triple of blocks
G
contains a permutation, that is a product of two
3
-cycles, permuting these blocks and fixing all the other points.
With this we get a set of even generators for the group
G
, which shows that
G
is even.
∎

3.
Imprimitive string C-groups of rank
r\geq n/2

Consider a string C-group
\Gamma=(G,S)
of rank
r
, where
G
is a transitive proper subgroup of
\mathrm{S}_{n}
, which is neither
\mathrm{S}_{n}
nor
\mathrm{A}_{n}
.
We will consider from now on that
r\geq n/2
. The case where
G

is primitive is addressed in Proposition
2.15
; therefore, from from now on
G
is an imprimitive group with
m
blocks of imprimitivity, each of size
k
.
In what follows let
\mathcal{G}
be the permutation representation graph of
G
.

As
G
is imprimitive, we can separate the set of generators
S
into the following three disjoint sets:

•

L
- the set of generators of
G
that define an independent generating set for the action of
G
on the set of
m
blocks;

•

C
- the set of generators of
G
which commute with all the elements of
L
; and

•

R
- the remaining set of generators of
G
that is in neither
L
nor
C
.

In what follows, we provide upper bounds for the sizes of
L
,
C
, and
R
. These results will allow us to conclude that either
k=2
or
m=2
. We treat these cases separately in Sections
4
and
5
, respectively.

The next lemma establishes a bound for
|C|
. We recall its proof here.

Lemma 3.1
.

[
4
, Section 2]
.
If
\langle L\rangle
acts primitively on the blocks, then
|C|\leq k-1
.

Proof.

Suppose that all the elements of
C
fix the blocks.
Since each element of
C
commutes with all elements of
L
, the permutation representation graph of

\langle C\rangle
consists of
m
disjoint copies (one per block) of a permutation representation graph of degree

k
. Hence
\langle C\rangle
acts faithfully on a block.
Hence
\langle C\rangle\leq\mathrm{S}_{k}
and therefore
|C|\leq k-1
, as wanted.
Consider the general case where the elements of
C
do not necessarily fix the blocks.

Suppose first that
m>2
and let
f:G\to\mathrm{S}_{m}
be the homomorphism induced by the action of
G
on the
m
blocks. If
\alpha\in C
is an element that permutes the blocks,
f(\alpha)
is a central involution in
\textrm{Im}(f)
. Hence, the orbits of the cyclic group
\langle f(\alpha)\rangle
form a nontrivial block system for

\textrm{Im}(f)
, contradicting the assumption that

\textrm{Im}(f)
is primitive.
Thus for
m>2
,
|C|\leq k-1
.

Now suppose that
m=2
and let
L=\{\alpha\}
.
Consider the endomorphism of
G
, defined by the correspondence
\rho\mapsto\bar{\rho}
, where
\bar{\rho}
is the permutation fixing the blocks defined as follows:

\bar{\rho}=\begin{cases}\rho\alpha&\text{if }\rho\text{ swaps the blocks},\\
\rho&\text{if }\rho\text{ fixes the blocks}.\end{cases}

This gives a one-to-one correspondence between
C
and
\bar{C}=\{\bar{\rho}_{i}\,|\,\rho_{i}\in C\}
.
Now
\langle\bar{C}\rangle\leq\mathrm{S}_{k}
.
Moreover if
\bar{\rho_{i}}\in\langle\rho_{j}\,|\,j\neq i\rangle
then
\rho_{i}\in\langle C\setminus\{\rho_{i}\}\rangle\langle L\rangle
, a contradiction.
Thus
\bar{C}
is an independent set of permutations in
\mathrm{S}_{k}
, hence
|C|=|\bar{C}|\leq k-1
.
∎

Lemma 3.2
.

If
m=4
and the action of
G
on blocks is
(C_{2})^{2}
, then
|C|\leq n/4-1
. Moreover we have the following:

(a)

If
|C|=n/4-1
then
\langle L\cup C\rangle\cong\mathrm{S}_{n/4}\times(C_{2})^{2}
; and

(b)

If
\gamma\in S
is a central involution fixing the blocks then
|C|\leq n/8
.

Proof.

Let
L=\{\alpha,\beta\}
. Now either
(\alpha\beta)^{2}
is trivial or not. Let us deal with these two cases separately.

(1) Suppose that there exists a set of nonconsecutive generators
L=\{\alpha,\beta\}
generating the block action.
Now consider the mapping
\rho\mapsto\bar{\rho}
where
\bar{\rho}
is a permutation fixing the blocks that is obtained by undoing the block action using elements of
\langle L\rangle
. That is,
\bar{\rho}=\rho\gamma
with
\gamma\in\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle
.
As
\bar{\rho}
centralizes
\langle L\rangle
, we conclude that
\langle\bar{\rho}\,|\,\rho\in C\rangle\leq\mathrm{S}_{n/4}
and
\{\bar{\rho}\,|\,\rho\in C\}
is independent, thus
|C|\leq n/4-1
.

When
|C|=n/4-1
,
\langle\bar{\rho}\,|\,\rho\in C\rangle\cong\mathrm{S}_{n/4}
, hence
\langle L\cup C\rangle\cong\mathrm{S}_{n/4}\times(C_{2})^{2}
.

If
\gamma\in S
is a central involution, particularly
\gamma\in C
, then
G\leq\mathrm{S}_{n/8}\times(C_{2})^{3}
with
L^{\prime}=\{\alpha,\beta,\gamma\}
generating the block action.
The set
C^{\prime}
of the elements commuting with the elements of
L^{\prime}
is equal to
C\setminus\{\gamma\}
. As before there is a natural embedding of
C\setminus\{\gamma\}
into
\mathrm{S}_{n/8}
.
Thus
|C\setminus\{\gamma\}|\leq n/8-1
, as wanted.

(2) Suppose there exist no pair
\{\alpha,\beta\}
generating the block action with
(\alpha\beta)^{2}
being trivial. Thus
L=\{\rho_{i},\rho_{i+1}\}
for some
i
. Then the elements of
C
must fix the blocks otherwise we are in case (1).
Hence
|C|\leq n/4-1
.

Clearly if
|C|=n/4-1
, then
\langle C\rangle=\mathrm{S}_{n/4}
. Finally if
\alpha
centralizes
\langle C\rangle
,
|C|\leq n/8
, as wanted.
∎

A block system
\{B_{1},\ldots,B_{m}\}
is
maximal block system
for
G
if there is no other block system having a block
X
(
|X|\neq n
) with
B_{1}\subseteq X
.

In what follows, we recall the results obtained in the proof of Proposition 2.1 of
[
4
]
.
We observe that in the proof of Proposition 2.1 of
[
4
]
, the lower bound on
n
given by the inequality
n/2\geq 5
was used to obtain a classification of the permutation representation graphs, rather than to bound the sets
L
,
R
, and
C
. As bounding these sets is the only focus of the following proposition, no condition on
n
is required here.

Proposition 3.3
.

[
4
]

Suppose that
L
,
C
and
R
are as defined above with respect to a maximal block system. Then these sets of generators satisfy the following properties:

(a)

\langle L\rangle
has a primitive action on the blocks;

(b)

|L|\leq m-1
;

(c)

If
|L|=m-1
and
m\geq 5
then the action of
\langle L\rangle
on the blocks corresponds to the standard Coxeter generators of
\mathrm{S}_{m}
;

(d)

If the set of labels of
L
is not an interval then
|L|\leq 2\log_{2}m
and
m\geq 60
, thus
|L|<m/4-3
;

(e)

If the set of labels of
L
is not an interval then
r<n/2
;

(f)

|C|\leq k-1
;

(g)

If the set of labels of the elements of
L
is an interval then
|R|\leq 2
; and

(h)

If
m\neq 2
and the set of labels of the elements of
L
is an interval, then
r\leq m+k-1
.

Corollary 3.4
.

If
r\geq n/2\geq 7
then one of the situations occurs:
k=2
,
m=2
.

Proof.

Suppose that
k,\,m\neq 2
.
By Proposition
3.3
(e) we may assume that the set of labels of the elements of
L
is an interval. Then by Proposition
3.3
(h),
r\leq m+k-1
. Thus
\frac{mk}{2}\leq m+k-1
, which is equivalent to
(m-2)(k-2)\leq 2
.
The latter inequality holds for
n\geq 14
.
∎

In most cases, we impose a lower bound
n\geq 14
to ensure the analysis remains within the two main cases under consideration (
k=2
and
m=2
). However, this bound is not always required. Whenever it is necessary, we will state it explicitly.

4.
Case: Imprimitive groups with blocks of size
2
.

In this case we consider that
G
has a maximal block system with blocks of size two, which implies that the action of
G
on the blocks is primitive.
Consider the sets
S
,
L
,
C
and
R
as in the previous section. Let us assume that
r=|S|\geq n/2
.
Then by Proposition
3.3
the set of labels of
L
is an interval and as
m\neq 2
,
|C|\leq 1
. Furthermore, if
C
is nonempty then the element of
C
is the permutation swapping the two points in every block.

In what follows let
\Psi
be the sggi corresponding to the action of
L
on the blocks.
As
\Psi
is primitive, by Lemma
2.7
, one of the following situations must occur:

|L|\leq n/2-3
,
\Psi
is on the the groups of Table
1
or
\Psi\in\{\mathrm{S}_{n/2},\mathrm{A}_{n/2}\}
. In the following proposition we rule out the groups of Table
1
.

Proposition 4.1
.

If
r\geq n/2
then
\Psi
cannot be isomorphic to one of the groups
\mathrm{D}_{10}
,
\mathrm{PGL}_{2}(5)
or
\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(5)
.

Proof.

Suppose that
\Psi
is one of the groups excluded in this proposition.
By Theorem
2.2

|L|\leq n/2-2
. In these cases
|R\cup C|=3
. Up to duality we may assume that
C=\{\rho_{0}\}
and
R=\{\rho_{1},\rho_{r-1}\}
.
Notice that
G_{i,j}<G_{i}<G
,
G_{i,j}<G_{j}<G
and
G_{i}\neq G_{j}
. However
G_{i}
and
G_{j}
might be isomorphic, since the corresponding permutation representations, can give distinct subgraphs of
\mathcal{G}
.

Let us consider each group,
\mathrm{PGL}_{2}(5)
,
\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(5)
and
\mathrm{D}_{10}
, separately.

(1)
\Psi\cong\mathrm{PGL}_{2}(5)
:
The following diagram gives the subgroups of
\mathrm{C}_{2}\wr\mathrm{PGL}_{2}(5)
which have
\Psi
as the block action.
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{C}_{2}\wr\mathrm{PGL}_{2}(5)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{C}_{2}^{5}.\mathrm{PGL}_{2}(5)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathrm{C}_{2}\wr\mathrm{PGL}_{2}(5))^{+}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{A}_{5}:\mathrm{C}_{4}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{C}_{2}\times\mathrm{PGL}_{2}(5)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{PGL}_{2}(5)}

Suppose first that
|R\cup C|=3
.
As
\mathrm{PGL}_{2}(5)
is almost simple and
G_{1,r-1}=\langle L\rangle\times\langle C\rangle
,
G_{1,r-1}\ncong\mathrm{PGL}_{2}(5)
.
Hence
G_{1,r-1}
is either
\mathrm{A}_{5}:\mathrm{C}_{4}
or
\mathrm{C}_{2}\times\mathrm{PGL}_{2}(5)
.
Then
G_{1}\cong G_{r-1}\cong\mathrm{C}_{2}^{5}.\mathrm{PGL}_{2}(5)
or
G_{1}\cong G_{r-1}\cong(\mathrm{C}_{2}\wr\mathrm{PGL}_{2}(5))^{+}
. But these groups have a unique transitive permutation representation on
12
points that is represented as a subgraph of
\mathcal{G}
, which gives
G_{1}=G_{r-1}
, a contradiction.
Thus
|R\cup C|=2
.

Now let
R\cup C=\{\rho_{i},\rho_{j}\}
. To avoid the previous contradiction we must have
G_{i,j}\cong\Psi\cong\mathrm{PGL}_{2}(5)
which might be transitive or intransitive (this is the only group of the diagram above that might be intransitive).
But then either
G_{i}\cong G_{j}\cong\mathrm{C}_{2}\times\mathrm{PGL}_{2}(5)
or
G_{i}\cong G_{j}\cong(\mathrm{C}_{2}\wr\mathrm{PGL}_{2}(5))^{+}
.
In the first case there is a central involution
\delta\in G_{i}\cap G_{j}=G_{i,j}\cong\mathrm{PGL}_{2}(5)
, a contradiction.
In the second case implies
G_{i}=G_{j}
, a contradiction.

(2)
\Psi\cong\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(5)
: Consider the case where
|R\cup C|=3
.
The transitive subgroups of
\mathrm{C}_{2}\wr\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(5)
having block action
\Psi
are the following:

\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(5)<\mathrm{C}_{2}\times\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(5)<(\mathrm{C}_{2}\wr\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(5))^{+}<\mathrm{C}_{2}\wr\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(5)

In this case we must have
G_{1,r-1}\cong\mathrm{C}_{2}\times\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(5)
and
G_{1}\cong G_{r-1}
.
But this gives
G_{1}=G_{r-1}
, a contradiction.
Then
|R\cup C|=2
. In this case, let
R=\{\rho_{i}\}
and
C=\{\rho_{j}\}
. This implies that
G_{i}\cong\mathrm{C}_{2}\times\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(5)
and
G_{j}
cannot be isomorphic to
\mathrm{C}_{2}\times\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(5)
. Hence either
G_{j}
is a proper subgroup of
G_{i}
or
G_{i}
is a proper subgroup of
G_{j}
, a contradiction.

(3)
\Psi\cong\mathrm{D}_{10}
: In this case
|R\cup C|=3
.
The transitive subgroups of
\mathrm{C}_{2}\wr\mathrm{D}_{10}
having block action
\Psi
are the following:

\mathrm{D}_{10};\,\mathrm{C}_{2}\times\mathrm{D}_{10};\,(\mathrm{C}_{2}\wr\mathrm{D}_{10})^{+};\,\mathrm{C}_{2}\wr\mathrm{D}_{10}

Then
G_{1}\cong\mathrm{C}_{2}\times\mathrm{D}_{10}
. If
G_{0}\cong G_{r-1}\cong(\mathrm{C}_{2}\wr\mathrm{D}_{10})^{+}
then
G_{0}=G_{r-1}
, a contradiction.
∎

By Proposition
3.3
, we have
|R\cup C|\leq 3
. We now show that if equality holds, then the group action on the blocks contains the alternating group.

Proposition 4.2
.

Let
r\geq n/2
.
If
|R\cup C|=3
then
\Psi
is isomorphic to
\mathrm{S}_{n/2}
or
\mathrm{A}_{n/2}
.

Proof.

Up to duality we may assume that
C=\{\rho_{0}\}
,
R=\{\rho_{1},\rho_{r-1}\}
and
L=\{\rho_{2},\ldots,\rho_{r-2}\}
.
Let
\alpha_{i}
be the action of
\rho_{i}
on the blocks. Then
\Psi=\langle\alpha_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{r-2}\rangle
.
Here we consider the following notation
\Psi_{i}:=\langle\alpha_{j}\,|\,j\neq i\rangle
.

Let us prove that
\Psi_{i}
is intransitive for every
i\in\{2,\ldots,r-2\}
when
n/2\geq 9
.

(1)
\Psi_{2}
and
\Psi_{r-2}
are intransitive:
Suppose that
\Psi_{2}
is transitive.
If
\rho_{1}
swaps a pair of points inside a block, then, as
\rho_{1}
centralizes
G_{>2}
,
\rho_{1}=\rho_{0}
, a contradiction.
Thus
\rho_{1}
swaps a pair of blocks. Then, the transitivity of
G_{>2}
, forces
\rho_{1}
to swap all blocks pair wisely.
Moreover
\rho_{0}\rho_{1}
is also a permutation swapping all blocks pair wisely and
\rho_{0}\rho_{1}\in\langle L\rangle
, a contradiction.
Therefore
\Psi_{2}
is intransitive and by duality
\Psi_{r-2}
is also intransitive.

(2)
\Psi_{i}
is intransitive for
i\in\{3,\ldots,r-3\}
:
Suppose that
\Psi_{i}\,(=\Psi_{\{2,\ldots,i-1\}}\times\Psi_{\{i+1,\ldots,r-2\}})
is transitive.
As
\Psi_{\{2,\ldots,i-1\}}\leq\Psi_{r-2}
and
\Psi_{\{i+1,\ldots,r-2\}}\leq\Psi_{2}
, these groups of the decomposition of
\Psi_{i}
are intransitive, by (1).
Hence
\Psi_{i}
is imprimitive.
Then, by Lemma
2.8
,
r-4=|L|-1\leq(k^{\prime}-1)+(m^{\prime}-1)
. As
|L|\geq n/2-3
, it follows that
n/2-4\leq k^{\prime}+m^{\prime}-2
, which is only possible if
(k^{\prime}-1)(m^{\prime}-1)\leq 3
, which is never the case as
n/2=k^{\prime}m^{\prime}\geq 9
.
This proves that for
i\in\{2,\dots,r-2\}
,
\Psi_{i}
is intransitive.
Now if
n/2\geq 2.3+3=9
, by Proposition
2.14
,
\Psi\cong\mathrm{S}_{n/2}
, as wanted.

Now suppose that
n/2\leq 8
. As
|L|\geq n/2-3
, by Lemma
2.7
and Proposition
4.1

\Psi
is either isomorphic to
\mathrm{S}_{n/2}
or to
\mathrm{A}_{n/2}
.
∎

However, as we show in the following proposition, the case
\Psi\in\{\mathrm{S}_{n/2},\mathrm{A}_{n/2}\}
also leads to a contradiction. Consequently, we obtain an improved upper bound on the size of
R\cup C
, and a corresponding lower bound on the size of
L
.

Proposition 4.3
.

If
r\geq n/2
then
|R\cup C|<3
and
|L|\geq n/2-2
.

Proof.

Suppose that
|R\cup C|=3
.
Then, by Proposition
4.2
,
\Psi
is isomorphic to
\mathrm{S}_{n/2}
or
\mathrm{A}_{n/2}
.
First consider the case
\Psi\cong\mathrm{S}_{n/2}
.
Then
C=\{\rho_{0}\}
and
R=\{\rho_{1},\rho_{r-1}\}
.
As
L
is a minimal set of generators generating the block action, and
G
is transitive,
G_{0,1}=\langle L\cup\{\rho_{r-1}\}\rangle
is transitive.
Hence the group
G_{0,1}
is a transitive subgroup of
\mathrm{C}_{2}\wr\mathrm{S}_{n/2}
.
Then, by Theorem
2.22
,
G_{0}
and
G_{1}
must be index
2
subgroups of
\mathrm{C}_{2}\wr\mathrm{S}_{n/2}
, while the order of
G_{0,1}
is twice that of
\mathrm{S}_{n/2}
.
Moreover,
G_{0,1}
contains
\rho_{0}
, the permutation swapping all points within the blocks, a contradiction.
The same argument can be applied when
\Psi
is isomorphic to
\mathrm{A}_{n/2}
.

Now as
|R\cup C|\leq 2
, it follows that
|L|=r-|R\cup C|\geq n/2-2
.
∎

In what follows, we consider the cases
|R\cup C|=2
and
|R\cup C|=1
. Proposition
4.4
and Corollary
4.5
address the case
|R\cup C|=2
.

Proposition 4.4
.

Let
r\geq n/2
. If
|R\cup C|=2
then
\langle L\rangle\cong\mathrm{S}_{n/2}
,
|R|=|C|=1
,
n/2
is odd and
G\cong\mathrm{C}_{2}\wr\mathrm{S}_{n/2}
.

Proof.

In this case
|L|\geq n/2-2
, hence
\Psi
is a transitive sggi of rank
n/2-2
and degree
n/2
. Suppose that
\Psi
is neither
\mathrm{S}_{n/2}
nor
\mathrm{A}_{n/2}
. Hence by Lemma
2.7

\Psi
is isomorphic to
one of the groups
\mathrm{D}_{10}
,
\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(5)
or
\mathrm{PGL}_{2}(5)
. But Proposition
4.1
excludes the possibility of
\Psi
being isomorphic to these groups.
Hence
\langle L\rangle
is a subgroup of
\mathrm{C}_{2}\wr H
with
H
being
\mathrm{A}_{n/2}
or
\mathrm{S}_{n/2}
.
Let us now use Theorem
2.22
to conclude that
\langle L\rangle\cong H
.
As
\langle L\rangle=G_{i,j}
for some
i
and
j
,
\langle L\rangle
cannot be an index
2
subgroup of
\mathrm{C}_{2}\wr H
.
Suppose that
|\langle L\rangle|=2|H|
. Then for distinct
i
and
j
the subgroups
G_{i}
and
G_{j}
must be index
2
subgroups of
\mathrm{C}_{2}\wr H
.
Thus
G_{i}
and
G_{j}
, and consequently
\langle L\rangle
, contain all even permutations fixing the blocks, a contradiction.
Hence
\langle L\rangle\cong H
, as wanted.

Now suppose that
H\cong\mathrm{A}_{n/2}
.
Then
\Psi\cong\mathrm{A}_{n/2}\cong\langle L\rangle
, thus
\Psi
is a string C-group. Hence we can use Theorem
2.16
to conclude that
|L|\leq\frac{n/2+1}{2}
. This implies that
n/2\leq 5
.
The only alternating group of degree at most
5
that is a string C-group is
\mathrm{A}_{5}
. This implies that
\langle L\rangle\cong\Psi\cong\mathrm{A}_{5}
.
But
\mathrm{A}_{5}
does not have a transitive imprimitive permutation representation on
10
points, a contradiction.

Suppose that
R\cup C=\{\rho_{i},\rho_{j}\}
.
As
G_{i,j}
does not contain a nontrivial permutation fixing all blocks, we may consider that,
G_{i,j}
is an index
2
subgroup of
G_{j}
and
G_{i}
is an index
2
subgroup of
\mathrm{C}_{2}\wr\mathrm{S}_{n/2}
.
Hence
\rho_{i}
commutes with all the elements of
G_{i,j}
, thus
\rho_{i}\in C
.
Moreover as
G_{j}
is not a subgroup of
G_{i}
,
\rho_{i}
must be an odd permutation, which is only possible if
n/2
is odd.
We also conclude that
G\cong\mathrm{C}_{2}\wr\mathrm{S}_{n/2}

The rest follows from Theorems
2.11
and
2.12
.
∎

Now using Proposition
4.4
it is possible to determine the possibilities for the permutation representation graph of
\Gamma
when
|R\cup C|=2
.

Corollary 4.5
.

Let
r\geq n/2\geq 7
. If
|R\cup C|=2
then
n/2
is odd and
\mathcal{G}
is, up to duality, one of the graphs of Table
5
(located at the end of the paper).

Proof.

Using Proposition
4.4
, it is possible to determine the possibilities for the permutation representation graph of
G
when
|R\cup C|=2
.

Up to duality we may assume that
C=\{\rho_{0}\}
where
\rho_{0}
is the permutation swapping all pairs of points within a block,
say
\rho_{0}=(1,2)\ldots(n-1,n)
.
Then either
R=\{\rho_{1}\}
or
R=\{\rho_{r-1}\}
.

We also need to consider two possibilities, either
|L|=n/2-1
or
|L|=n/2-2
. When
|L|=n/2-1
,
\langle L\rangle
is the automorphism group of the simplex, which is self dual;
when
|L|=n/2-2
,
\langle L\rangle
is the automorphism group of one of the two abstract regular polytopes of rank
n/2-2
for
\mathrm{S}_{n/2}
, having one of the Schläfli symbol
\{3,\ldots,3,6,4\}
or
\{4,6,3,\ldots,3\}
(which are dual to each other).
Finally, the possibilities for the permutation representation graph of these polytopes are determined by Propositions
2.13
and
2.19
, depending on whether
\langle L\rangle
is intransitive or transitive. If
\langle L\rangle
is intransitive, the permutation graph of
\langle L\rangle
is given by two copies of one of the graphs given in Proposition
2.13
.
The possibilities for the element of
R
, which must be an even permutation, are determined by the commuting property.
With this we obtain the graphs listed in Table
5
.
∎

It remains to consider the case
|R\cup C|=1
. In what follows, we determine the action on the blocks. To achieve this, a lower bound on
n
is required.

Proposition 4.6
.

Let
r\geq n/2\geq 7
. If
|R\cup C|=1
then
\Psi\cong\mathrm{S}_{n/2}
and the action of
G
on the blocks is given by the following graph:

\scriptstyle{1}
\scriptstyle{r-1}

Proof.

In this case
|L|\geq n/2-1
, hence
\Psi
is a transitive sggi of rank
n/2-1
and degree
n/2
. Up to duality we may assume that
R\cup C=\{\rho_{0}\}
.
By Corollary
2.10
,
\Psi\cong\mathrm{S}_{n/2}
and the block action graph is the one given in the statement of this proposition.

∎

We now determine the action of
\langle L\rangle
. Either
\langle L\rangle\cong\mathrm{S}_{n/2}
or
\langle L\rangle\not\cong\mathrm{S}_{n/2}
, and the following proposition addresses the former case.

Proposition 4.7
.

Let
r\geq n/2\geq 7
. If
|R\cup C|=1
and
\langle L\rangle\cong\mathrm{S}_{n/2}
then
\mathcal{G}
is one of the graphs of Table
6
or the graphs (1) and (2) of Table
9
.

Proof.

First, consider the case where
\langle L\rangle
is intransitive.
Then
\langle L\rangle
is represented by two copies of the permutation graph of the symplex.
Suppose first that
|C|=1
. In this case
G\cong\mathrm{C}_{2}\times\mathrm{S}_{n/2}
and
G
admits another block system with exactly two blocks. The permutation representation graph of
\Gamma
is the graph (1) appearing on Table
9
.
If
|C|=0
then we get, up to duality, the permutation representation graphs (13) and (14) of Table
6
.

Consider now that
\langle L\rangle
is transitive. In this case the permutation representation of
\langle L\rangle
is given by Proposition
2.19
.
If
|C|=1
then
G\cong\mathrm{C}_{2}\times\mathrm{S}_{n/2}
and
G
admits another block system with exactly two blocks. The permutation representation graph of
\Gamma
is the graph (2) of Table
9
.
If
|C|=0
then, we get the graphs (15) and (16) of Table
6
.
∎

In what remains of this section assume the following:

•

r\geq n/2\geq 7
;

•

R\cup C=\{\rho_{0}\}
;

•

\langle L\rangle\not\cong\mathrm{S}_{n/2}
; and

•

f:\langle L\rangle\rightarrow\mathrm{S}_{n/2}
, the homomorphism induced by the action of
\langle L\rangle
on the
n/2
blocks.

By Proposition
4.6
the permutation representation graph of
\Psi
determines a natural ordering on the blocks: let
B_{1}
be the first block (on the left) and
B_{n/2}
be the last block (on the right).
Considering
f:\langle L\rangle\rightarrow\mathrm{S}_{n/2}
as above, by Theorem
2.20
, either
{\rm Ker}(f)\cong\mathrm{C}_{2}
,
{\rm Ker}(f)\cong(\mathrm{C}_{2})^{n/2-1}
or
{\rm Ker}(f)\cong(\mathrm{C}_{2})^{n/2}
.
However, it cannot be
{\rm Ker}(f)\cong(\mathrm{C}_{2})^{n/2}
, since this would imply that
\langle L\rangle
is the full wreath product
\mathrm{C}_{2}\wr\mathrm{S}_{n/2}
, which is not possible because
R\cup C
is nonempty.
We will represent each element
\alpha\in{\rm Ker}(f)
as a vector
\tilde{\alpha}\in\{0,1\}^{n/2}
.
Particularly, the central involution permuting all pairs of points within a block corresponds to the all 1’s vector.
In what follows consider the following notation where
x^{i}
represents a sequence of length
i
of
x
’s, say (
x,\,x,\ldots,\,x
),
x\in\{0,1\}
.

\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathcal{O}:=(0^{r})&\mbox{all 0's vector}\\
\mathcal{U}:=(1^{r})&\mbox{all 1's vector}\\
\mathcal{L}_{i}:=(1^{i},0^{r-i})&\mbox{a left-side 1's vector when }i\in\{1,\ldots,r-1\};\,\mathcal{L}_{0}:=\mathcal{O}\mbox{ and }\mathcal{L}_{r}:=\mathcal{U}\\
\mathcal{R}_{i}:=(0^{i},1^{r-i})&\mbox{a right-side 1's vector when }i\in\{1,\ldots,r-1\};\,\mathcal{R}_{r}:=\mathcal{O}\mbox{ and }\mathcal{R}_{0}:=\mathcal{U}\\
\mathcal{V}_{i}:=(1^{i},0,0,1^{r-(i+2)})&\mbox{a 2-central 0's vector when }i\in\{1,\ldots,r-3\};\,\mathcal{V}_{0}:=\mathcal{R}_{2};\,\mathcal{V}_{r-2}:=\mathcal{L}_{r-2}\\
\mathcal{T}_{i}:=(1^{i},0,0,0,1^{r-(i+3)})&\mbox{a 3-central 0's vector when }i\in\{1,\ldots,r-4\}\};\,\mathcal{T}_{0}:=\mathcal{R}_{3};\,\mathcal{T}_{r-3}:=\mathcal{L}_{r-3};\\
\end{array}

For
i\in\{1,\ldots,r-1\}
let
\rho_{i}=\alpha_{i}\beta_{i}
with
\alpha_{i}
being a permutation fixing the blocks and
\beta_{i}
being the permutation swapping
B_{i}
and
B_{i+1}
.
Then thanks to the commuting property,
\tilde{\alpha}_{i}
is either
\mathcal{O}
,
\mathcal{L}_{i-1}
,
\mathcal{R}_{i+1}
or
\mathcal{V}_{i-1}
.

Let
\delta_{i}:=(\rho_{i}\rho_{i+1})^{3}
(
i>0
). In the following table we determine all the possibilities for
\tilde{\delta}_{i}
. As
\delta_{i}\in{\rm Ker}(f)
and
{\rm Ker}(f)\cong\mathrm{C}_{2}
or
{\rm Ker}(f)\cong(\mathrm{C}_{2})^{n/2-1}
,
\delta_{i}=(\rho_{i}\rho_{i+1})^{3}
is either the permutation
(1,2)\ldots(n-1,n)
or an even permutation.
In the following table we determine all the possibilities for
\tilde{\delta}_{i}
for all the possibilities for the pair
(\tilde{\alpha}_{i},\tilde{\alpha}_{i+1})
.
In some cases the result is an odd permutation, thus these cases cannot happen (otherwise
\langle L\rangle
is the full wreath product
\mathrm{C}_{2}\wr\mathrm{S}_{n/2}
, implying that
\rho_{0}\in\langle L\rangle
, a contradiction).

i\in\{2,\ldots,r-3\}

i=1

i=r-2

\tilde{\alpha_{i}}\backslash\tilde{\alpha}_{i+1}

\mathcal{O}

\mathcal{L}_{i}

\mathcal{R}_{i+2}

\mathcal{O}

\mathcal{R}_{i+2}

\mathcal{U}

\mathcal{T}_{i-1}

\mathcal{T}_{i-1}

\mathcal{V}_{i-1}

\mathcal{U}

\mathcal{O}

\tilde{\alpha_{1}}\backslash\tilde{\alpha}_{2}

\mathcal{O}

\mathcal{L}_{1}

\mathcal{V}_{1}

\mathcal{O}

\mathcal{R}_{3}

\mathcal{U}
